It is easy to see that the three listed binary relations are symmetric and transitive relations, but they are not equivalence relations. assistant: ```python MOD = 10**9 + 7 n = int(input().strip()) if n == 0: print(0) else: # Precompute factorial and inverse factorial arrays up to n fact = [1] * (n + 1) for i in range(1, n + 1): fact[i] = fact[i-1] * i % MOD inv_fact = [1] * (n + 1) inv_fact[n] = pow(fact[n], MOD-2, MOD) for i in range(n-1, -1, -1): inv_fact[i] = inv_fact[i+1] * (i+1) % MOD # Function to compute combination C(n, k) def comb(a, b): if b < 0 or b > a: return 0 return fact[a] * inv_fact[b] % MOD * inv_fact[a - b] % MOD # Compute Bell numbers up to n Bell = [0] * (n + 1) Bell[0] = 1 for m in range(1, n + 1): total = 0 m_prev = m - 1 a_fact = fact[m_prev] for k in range(0, m): c = a_fact * inv_fact[k] % MOD c = c * inv_fact[m_prev - k] % MOD total = (total + c * Bell[k]) % MOD Bell[m] = total # Calculate the answer: sum_{k=0}^{n-1} C(n, k) * Bell[k] ans = 0 for k in range(0, n): c = comb(n, k) ans = (ans + c * Bell[k]) % MOD print(ans % MOD) ```	user: Little Johnny has recently learned about set theory. Now he is studying binary relations. You've probably heard the term "equivalence relation". These relations are very important in many areas of mathematics. For example, the equality of the two numbers is an equivalence relation. A set ρ of pairs (a, b) of elements of some set A is called a binary relation on set A. For two elements a and b of the set A we say that they are in relation ρ, if pair <image>, in this case we use a notation <image>. Binary relation is equivalence relation, if: 1. It is reflexive (for any a it is true that <image>); 2. It is symmetric (for any a, b it is true that if <image>, then <image>); 3. It is transitive (if <image> and <image>, than <image>). Little Johnny is not completely a fool and he noticed that the first condition is not necessary! Here is his "proof": Take any two elements, a and b. If <image>, then <image> (according to property (2)), which means <image> (according to property (3)). It's very simple, isn't it? However, you noticed that Johnny's "proof" is wrong, and decided to show him a lot of examples that prove him wrong. Here's your task: count the number of binary relations over a set of size n such that they are symmetric, transitive, but not an equivalence relations (i.e. they are not reflexive). Since their number may be very large (not 0, according to Little Johnny), print the remainder of integer division of this number by 109 + 7. Input A single line contains a single integer n (1 ≤ n ≤ 4000). Output In a single line print the answer to the problem modulo 109 + 7. Examples Input 1 Output 1 Input 2 Output 3 Input 3 Output 10 Note If n = 1 there is only one such relation — an empty one, i.e. <image>. In other words, for a single element x of set A the following is hold: <image>. If n = 2 there are three such relations. Let's assume that set A consists of two elements, x and y. Then the valid relations are <image>, ρ = {(x, x)}, ρ = {(y, y)}. It is easy to see that the three listed binary relations are symmetric and transitive relations, but they are not equivalence relations. assistant: To solve this problem, we need to count the number of binary relations over a set of size \( n \) that are symmetric, transitive, but not equivalence relations. The key steps involve understanding that such relations correspond to subsets of the set equipped with an equivalence relation on those subsets. The solution involves computing Bell numbers and binomial coefficients modulo \( 10^9 + 7 \). ### Approach 1. **Understanding Requirements**: - A symmetric and transitive relation can be seen as a union of equivalence relations over its subset of elements. - Equivalence relations (which are reflexive) need to be excluded from our count. 2. **Bell Numbers**: - Bell numbers \( B(n) \) count the number of ways to partition a set of \( n \) elements. They are essential here as each subset's equivalence relations count contributes to the total. 3. **Binomial Coefficients**: - We need binomial coefficients to count how many ways subsets of elements can be chosen from the set \( n \). 4. **Dynamic Programming Approach**: - Precompute factorials and their modular inverses to efficiently compute binomial coefficients. - Compute Bell numbers using the recurrence relation \( B(m) = \sum_{k=0}^{m-1} \binom{m-1}{k} B(k) \). 5. **Final Calculation**: - Sum contributions from all subsets of size \( k \) (from 0 to \( n-1 \)), each contributing \( \binom{n}{k} \times B(k) \). ### Solution Code ```cpp #include <iostream> #include <vector> using namespace std; typedef long long ll; const int MOD = 1e9 + 7; int main() { int n; cin >> n; vector<ll> fact(n + 1), inv_fact(n + 1); fact[0] = 1; for (int i = 1; i <= n; ++i) { fact[i] = fact[i - 1] * i % MOD; } inv_fact[n] = 1; inv_fact[n] = pow(fact[n], MOD - 2, MOD); for (int i = n - 1; i >= 0; --i) { inv_fact[i] = inv_fact[i + 1] * (i + 1) % MOD; } vector<ll> B(n + 1); B[0] = 1; for (int m = 1; m <= n; ++m) { ll total = 0; for (int k = 0; k < m; ++k) { int a = m - 1; int b = k; int c = a - b; ll denom = inv_fact[b] * inv_fact[c] % MOD; ll comb = fact[a] * denom % MOD; ll term = comb * B[k] % MOD; total = (total + term) % MOD; } B[m] = total; } ll ans = 0; for (int k = 0; k < n; ++k) { ll comb = fact[n]; comb = comb * inv_fact[k] % MOD; comb = comb * inv_fact[n - k] % MOD; ll term = comb * B[k] % MOD; ans = (ans + term) % MOD; } cout << ans << endl; return 0; } ``` ### Explanation 1. **Factorial and Inverse Factorial Arrays**: - `fact` array stores factorials modulo \( 10^9 + 7 \). - `inv_fact` array stores modular inverses of factorials to compute binomial coefficients efficiently. 2. **Bell Numbers Calculation**: - Using dynamic programming, `B[m]` is computed using the recurrence relation involving binomial coefficients and previous Bell numbers. 3. **Final Summation**: - The result is the sum over all subset sizes \( k \) from 0 to \( n-1 \), computed using binomial coefficients and corresponding Bell numbers, ensuring non-equivalence by excluding the entire set. This approach efficiently computes the required values using modular arithmetic to handle large numbers, ensuring correctness within the problem constraints.	user: Little Johnny has recently learned about set theory. Now he is studying binary relations. You've probably heard the term "equivalence relation". These relations are very important in many areas of mathematics. For example, the equality of the two numbers is an equivalence relation. A set ρ of pairs (a, b) of elements of some set A is called a binary relation on set A. For two elements a and b of the set A we say that they are in relation ρ, if pair $(a, b) \in \rho$, in this case we use a notation $a \stackrel{\rho}{\sim} b$. Binary relation is equivalence relation, if: It is reflexive (for any a it is true that $a \stackrel{\rho}{\sim} a$); It is symmetric (for any a, b it is true that if $a \stackrel{\rho}{\sim} b$, then $b \stackrel{\rho}{\sim} a$); It is transitive (if $a \stackrel{\rho}{\sim} b$ and $b \stackrel{\rho}{\sim} c$, than $a \stackrel{\rho}{\sim} c$). Little Johnny is not completely a fool and he noticed that the first condition is not necessary! Here is his "proof": Take any two elements, a and b. If $a \stackrel{\rho}{\sim} b$, then $b \stackrel{\rho}{\sim} a$ (according to property (2)), which means $a \stackrel{\rho}{\sim} a$ (according to property (3)). It's very simple, isn't it? However, you noticed that Johnny's "proof" is wrong, and decided to show him a lot of examples that prove him wrong. Here's your task: count the number of binary relations over a set of size n such that they are symmetric, transitive, but not an equivalence relations (i.e. they are not reflexive). Since their number may be very large (not 0, according to Little Johnny), print the remainder of integer division of this number by 10^9 + 7. -----Input----- A single line contains a single integer n (1 ≤ n ≤ 4000). -----Output----- In a single line print the answer to the problem modulo 10^9 + 7. -----Examples----- Input 1 Output 1 Input 2 Output 3 Input 3 Output 10 -----Note----- If n = 1 there is only one such relation — an empty one, i.e. $\rho = \varnothing$. In other words, for a single element x of set A the following is hold: [Image]. If n = 2 there are three such relations. Let's assume that set A consists of two elements, x and y. Then the valid relations are $\rho = \varnothing$, ρ = {(x, x)}, ρ = {(y, y)}. It is easy to see that the three listed binary relations are symmetric and transitive relations, but they are not equivalence relations. assistant: To solve this problem, we need to count the number of symmetric and transitive binary relations on a set of size \( n \) that are not equivalence relations. The key steps involve understanding the structure of such relations and efficiently computing the necessary values using dynamic programming and modular arithmetic. ### Approach 1. **Understanding the Problem**: - A symmetric and transitive relation that is not an equivalence relation must exclude at least one reflexive pair \((a, a)\). - The problem reduces to counting all symmetric and transitive relations and subtracting those that are equivalence relations (which include all reflexive pairs). 2. **Bell Numbers**: - Bell numbers count the number of ways to partition a set (equivalence relations). We need Bell numbers up to \( n \). 3. **Combinatorial Terms**: - Use factorial and inverse factorial arrays to efficiently compute binomial coefficients \( C(n, k) \). 4. **Dynamic Programming for Bell Numbers**: - Calculate Bell numbers using the recurrence relation \( \text{Bell}(m) = \sum_{k=0}^{m-1} C(m-1, k) \times \text{Bell}(k) \). 5. **Final Calculation**: - For each input \( n \), compute the total number of symmetric and transitive relations using the precomputed Bell numbers and subtract the number of equivalence relations. ### Solution Code ```python MOD = 10**9 + 7 max_n = 4000 # Precompute factorial and inverse factorial arrays fact = [1] * (max_n + 1) for i in range(1, max_n + 1): fact[i] = fact[i-1] * i % MOD inv_fact = [1] * (max_n + 1) inv_fact[max_n] = pow(fact[max_n], MOD-2, MOD) for i in range(max_n - 1, -1, -1): inv_fact[i] = inv_fact[i+1] * (i+1) % MOD # Precompute Bell numbers Bell = [0] * (max_n + 1) Bell[0] = 1 for m in range(1, max_n + 1): total = 0 a = m - 1 for k in range(m): c = fact[a] * inv_fact[k] % MOD c = c * inv_fact[a - k] % MOD term = c * Bell[k] % MOD total = (total + term) % MOD Bell[m] = total # Read input and compute answer n = int(input()) sum_val = 0 for k in range(0, n + 1): b_val = Bell[n - k] c_n_k = fact[n] * inv_fact[k] % MOD c_n_k = c_n_k * inv_fact[n - k] % MOD term = c_n_k * b_val % MOD sum_val = (sum_val + term) % MOD ans = (sum_val - Bell[n]) % MOD print(ans) ``` ### Explanation 1. **Factorial and Inverse Factorial Arrays**: - We precompute factorials and their modular inverses up to \( 4000 \) to efficiently compute binomial coefficients using the formula \( C(n, k) = \text{fact}[n] \times \text{inv\_fact}[k] \times \text{inv\_fact}[n-k] \mod MOD \). 2. **Bell Numbers**: - Using the recurrence relation, we compute Bell numbers up to \( 4000 \). Each Bell number is derived by summing contributions from all smaller partitions using binomial coefficients. 3. **Final Computation**: - For the input \( n \), we compute the total number of symmetric and transitive relations by summing \( C(n, k) \times \text{Bell}(n-k) \) for all \( k \) from \( 0 \) to \( n \). Subtract the number of equivalence relations (Bell(n)) to get the result. This approach efficiently handles the large input constraints using dynamic programming and modular arithmetic, ensuring correctness and performance.

Dima is sure that it'll be great to learn to solve the following problem by the Big Day: You're given a set A, consisting of numbers l, l + 1, l + 2, ..., r; let's consider all its k-element subsets; for each such subset let's find the largest common divisor of Fibonacci numbers with indexes, determined by the subset elements. Among all found common divisors, Dima is interested in the largest one. Dima asked to remind you that Fibonacci numbers are elements of a numeric sequence, where F1 = 1, F2 = 1, Fn = Fn - 1 + Fn - 2 for n ≥ 3. Dima has more than half a century ahead to solve the given task, but you only have two hours. Count the residue from dividing the sought largest common divisor by m. Input The first line contains four space-separated integers m, l, r and k (1 ≤ m ≤ 109; 1 ≤ l < r ≤ 1012; 2 ≤ k ≤ r - l + 1). Please, do not use the %lld specifier to read or write 64-bit integers in С++. It is preferred to use cin, cout streams or the %I64d specifier. Output Print a single integer — the residue from dividing the sought greatest common divisor by m. Examples Input 10 1 8 2 Output 3 Input 10 1 8 3 Output 1 assistant: ```python import sys def main(): m, l, r, k = map(int, sys.stdin.readline().split()) def count(d): if d == 0: return 0 return (r // d) - ((l - 1) // d) low = 1 high = r best_d = 0 while low <= high: mid = (low + high) // 2 cnt = count(mid) if cnt >= k: best_d = mid low = mid + 1 else: high = mid - 1 def compute_fib(n): if n <= 2: return 1 % m mat = [[1, 1], [1, 0]] power_needed = n - 2 def matrix_mult(a, b): new00 = (a[0][0] * b[0][0] + a[0][1] * b[1][0]) % m new01 = (a[0][0] * b[0][1] + a[0][1] * b[1][1]) % m new10 = (a[1][0] * b[0][0] + a[1][1] * b[1][0]) % m new11 = (a[1][0] * b[0][1] + a[1][1] * b[1][1]) % m return [[new00, new01], [new10, new11]] def matrix_pow(mat, power): result = [[1, 0], [0, 1]] while power > 0: if power % 2 == 1: result = matrix_mult(result, mat) mat = matrix_mult(mat, mat) power = power // 2 return result mat_pow = matrix_pow(mat, power_needed) return (mat_pow[0][0] + mat_pow[0][1]) % m print(compute_fib(best_d)) if __name__ == "__main__": main() ```	user: There are less than 60 years left till the 900-th birthday anniversary of a famous Italian mathematician Leonardo Fibonacci. Dima has more than half a century ahead to solve the given task, but you only have two hours. Count the residue from dividing the sought largest common divisor by m. Input The first line contains four space-separated integers m, l, r and k (1 ≤ m ≤ 109; 1 ≤ l < r ≤ 1012; 2 ≤ k ≤ r - l + 1). Please, do not use the %lld specifier to read or write 64-bit integers in С++. It is preferred to use cin, cout streams or the %I64d specifier. Output Print a single integer — the residue from dividing the sought greatest common divisor by m. Examples Input 10 1 8 2 Output 3 Input 10 1 8 3 Output 1 assistant: To solve this problem, we need to determine the largest common divisor (GCD) of Fibonacci numbers corresponding to elements of a k-element subset of the interval [l, r], then compute this GCD modulo m. The key steps involve finding the maximum possible GCD of subset elements and efficiently computing the Fibonacci number modulo m. ### Approach 1. **Finding the Maximum GCD (D)**: - We need to find the largest integer D such that there are at least k numbers in the interval [l, r] divisible by D. This is done using binary search. The count of numbers divisible by D in the interval can be quickly computed using integer division. 2. **Efficient Fibonacci Calculation**: - Once we determine D, the Fibonacci number F(D) modulo m is computed using a fast doubling method, which leverages mathematical properties to compute Fibonacci numbers in O(log D) time efficiently even for very large D. ### Solution Code ```cpp #include <iostream> #include <utility> using namespace std; typedef long long LL; LL m, l, r, k; LL compute_count(LL d) { if (d == 0) return 0; LL cnt_r = r / d; LL cnt_l = (l - 1) / d; return cnt_r - cnt_l; } LL find_max_D() { LL low = 1; LL high = r; LL max_d = 0; while (low <= high) { LL mid = low + (high - low) / 2; LL cnt = compute_count(mid); if (cnt >= k) { max_d = mid; low = mid + 1; } else { high = mid - 1; } } return max_d; } typedef pair<LL, LL> PII; PII fib_helper(LL n, LL mod) { if (n == 0) return {0, 1}; auto p = fib_helper(n / 2, mod); LL a = p.first, b = p.second; LL c = (a * (2 * b - a)) % mod; LL d = (a * a + b * b) % mod; if (n % 2 == 0) return {c, d}; else return {d, (c + d) % mod}; } LL fib_mod(LL n, LL mod) { if (n == 0) return 0; auto p = fib_helper(n - 1, mod); return p.second % mod; } int main() { cin >> m >> l >> r >> k; LL D = find_max_D(); LL res = fib_mod(D, m); cout << res << endl; return 0; } ``` ### Explanation 1. **Finding Maximum D (Binary Search)**: - The `find_max_D` function uses binary search to find the largest D such that there are at least k numbers in [l, r] divisible by D. The count of such numbers is computed using integer division, making the process efficient. 2. **Efficient Fibonacci Calculation**: - The `fib_mod` function uses a fast doubling method implemented via recursion. This method reduces the time complexity to O(log D) by leveraging properties of Fibonacci numbers. The function `fib_helper` recursively computes pairs of Fibonacci numbers to avoid redundant calculations. By combining these techniques, we efficiently determine the desired GCD and its corresponding Fibonacci number modulo m, ensuring the solution works within the problem constraints.	user: There are less than 60 years left till the 900-th birthday anniversary of a famous Italian mathematician Leonardo Fibonacci. Examples Input 10 1 8 2 Output 3 Input 10 1 8 3 Output 1 assistant: To solve this problem, we need to determine the largest possible greatest common divisor (GCD) of Fibonacci numbers corresponding to a subset of size \( k \) from the interval \([l, r]\), and then find the result modulo \( m \). ### Approach 1. **Understanding GCD of Fibonacci Numbers**: - The GCD of Fibonacci numbers \( F_a \) and \( F_b \) is \( F_{\text{gcd}(a, b)} \). This property extends to more elements, so the GCD of Fibonacci numbers at indices in a subset is \( F_{\text{gcd}(\text{elements of the subset})} \). 2. **Finding the Maximum GCD**: - We need to find the largest \( d \) such that there are at least \( k \) multiples of \( d \) in the interval \([l, r]\). This \( d \) will be the maximum possible GCD of a subset of size \( k \). 3. **Binary Search for Maximum \( d \)**: - Use binary search to determine the maximum \( d \) for which there are at least \( k \) multiples of \( d \) in \([l, r]\). The count of multiples of \( d \) in \([l, r]\) can be computed using integer division. 4. **Computing Fibonacci Number Modulo \( m \)**: - Once the maximum \( d \) is found, compute \( F(d) \mod m \) using fast doubling method, which allows efficient calculation of large Fibonacci numbers modulo \( m \) in \( O(\log d) \) time. ### Solution Code ```cpp #include <iostream> using namespace std; long long compute_count(long long d, long long l, long long r) { return (r / d) - ((l - 1) / d); } long long fibonacci(long long n, long long mod) { if (n == 0) return 0; auto f = [&](long long n, long long mod) -> pair<long long, long long> { if (n == 0) return {0LL, 1LL}; auto p = f(n / 2, mod); long long a = p.first, b = p.second; long long c = (a * ((2 * b - a) % mod)) % mod; long long d_val = (a * a + b * b) % mod; if (n % 2 == 0) { return {c, d_val}; } else { return {d_val, (c + d_val) % mod}; } }; return f(n, mod).first % mod; } int main() { long long m, l, r, k; cin >> m >> l >> r >> k; long long best_d = 0; long long low = 1, high = r; while (low <= high) { long long mid = (low + high) / 2; long long cnt = compute_count(mid, l, r); if (cnt >= k) { best_d = mid; low = mid + 1; } else { high = mid - 1; } } cout << fibonacci(best_d, m); return 0; } ``` ### Explanation 1. **Binary Search for Maximum \( d \)**: - We perform a binary search over possible values of \( d \) from 1 to \( r \). - For each \( d \), we compute how many numbers in \([l, r]\) are divisible by \( d \) using integer division. - Adjust the search range based on whether the count of multiples meets or exceeds \( k \). 2. **Fast Doubling Algorithm**: - This method efficiently computes Fibonacci numbers using the properties of Fibonacci number identities. It recursively computes pairs of Fibonacci numbers, halving the problem size each time, achieving logarithmic complexity. 3. **Modulo Operation**: - The Fibonacci computation is done modulo \( m \) to ensure we handle large numbers effectively and adhere to the problem constraints. This approach ensures that we efficiently find the required GCD and compute the result within acceptable time limits even for large values of \( r \) and \( m \).

Examples Input 3 3 1 3 1 Output 9 Input 5 5 1 1 4 2 2 Output 1000 Input 6 2 1 1 2 2 1 1 Output 16 Note For the first example, valid answer suits are [2,1,1], [2,1,2], [2,1,3], [3,1,1], [3,1,2], [3,1,3], [3,2,1], [3,2,2], [3,2,3]. assistant: ```python MOD = 998244353 def main(): import sys n, k = map(int, sys.stdin.readline().split()) h = list(map(int, sys.stdin.readline().split())) # Compute m: count of indices where h[i] != h[next] m = 0 for i in range(n): next_i = (i + 1) % n if h[i] != h[next_i]: m += 1 if m == 0: print(0) return # Precompute factorials and inverse factorials up to n max_fact = max(n, m) fact = [1] * (max_fact + 1) for i in range(1, max_fact + 1): fact[i] = fact[i-1] * i % MOD inv_fact = [1] * (max_fact + 1) inv_fact[max_fact] = pow(fact[max_fact], MOD-2, MOD) for i in range(max_fact-1, -1, -1): inv_fact[i] = inv_fact[i+1] * (i+1) % MOD # Precompute pow_table for (k-2)^exponent up to m pow_kminus2 = (k - 2) % MOD max_pow = m pow_table = [1] * (max_pow + 1) for i in range(1, max_pow + 1): pow_table[i] = pow_table[i-1] * pow_kminus2 % MOD # Compute c c = 0 for l in range(0, m + 1, 2): if l > m: continue # Compute C(m, l) cm_l = fact[m] * inv_fact[l] % MOD cm_l = cm_l * inv_fact[m - l] % MOD # Compute C(l, l//2) half = l // 2 cl_half = fact[l] * inv_fact[half] % MOD cl_half = cl_half * inv_fact[half] % MOD # Compute power term: (k-2)^(m - l) exp = m - l term_p = pow_table[exp] total_term = cm_l * cl_half % MOD total_term = total_term * term_p % MOD c = (c + total_term) % MOD total_mways = pow(k, m, MOD) inv2 = (MOD + 1) // 2 % MOD cnt = (total_mways - c) % MOD cnt = cnt * inv2 % MOD ans = cnt * pow(k, n - m, MOD) % MOD print(ans) if __name__ == "__main__": main() ```

If so, can this variant be solved efficiently, or is it known to be a computationally hard problem? assistant: This problem is indeed an NP-hard task. It can be proven by reducing it from the PARTITION problem, which is also NP-hard. Given a set of positive integers $S=\{x_1,\ldots,x_n\}$, we create a flow network as follows: Vertices: $V = \{s, v, t\} \cup \{x_1,\ldots,x_n\}$ Edges: $E = \{(s, x_i) | x_i\in S\} \cup \{(x_i, v)|x_i\in S\} \cup \{(v, t)\}$ Capacities: $c((s, x_i))=c((x_i, v))=x_i$ and $c((v, t))=\frac{\sum_{i\in[n]}{x_i}}{2}$ The PARTITION problem has a solution if and only if there exists a "saturate or avoid" flow in this network with a value equal to $c((v, t))$. Thus, finding such a flow in polynomial time is as hard as solving the PARTITION problem, which is known to be NP-complete.	user: How can I formulate a minimum clique cover problem as an integer programming problem? assistant: The objective function minimizes the number of cliques used to cover the graph. The first constraint ensures that a node is only in a clique if the clique is used. The second and third constraints ensure that an edge is in a clique if and only if both of its nodes are in the clique. The fourth and fifth constraints ensure that a clique can only contain nodes that are connected by an edge. The sixth constraint ensures that each edge is in exactly one clique. #### To formulate the minimum clique cover problem as an integer programming problem, you can use the following steps: 1. Define the decision variables: - $x_{i,c}$: Binary variable indicating whether node $i$ is in clique $c$. - $y_{i,j,c}$: Binary variable indicating whether edge $(i,j)$ is in clique $c$. - $z_c$: Binary variable indicating whether clique $c$ is used. 2. Define the objective function: - Minimize $\sum_c z_c$. 3. Define the constraints: - $x_{i,c} \le z_c$ for all $i$ and $c$. - $x_{i,c} + x_{j,c} -1 \le y_{i,j,c}$ for all $(i,j) \in E$ and $c$. - $x_{i,c} + x_{j,c} \le 1$ for all $(i,j) \not\in E$ and $c$. - $y_{i,j,c} \le x_{i,c}$ for all $(i,j) \in E$ and $c$. - $y_{i,j,c} \le x_{j,c}$ for all $(i,j) \in E$ and $c$. - $\sum_c y_{i,j,c} = 1$ for all $(i,j) \in E$.

Args: amount: Amount to deposit. Returns: None """ if amount > 0: self.balance += amount print(f"Deposit Successful. New balance: ${self.balance}") else: print("Invalid deposit amount.") def withdraw(self, amount: float) -> None: """ Withdraws a specified amount from the account. Args: amount: Amount to withdraw. Returns: None """ if amount > 0 and amount <= self.balance: self.balance -= amount print(f"Withdrawal Successful. New balance: ${self.balance}") elif amount > self.balance: print("Insufficient funds.") else: print("Invalid withdrawal amount.") def get_balance(self) -> float: """ Retrieves the current account balance. Returns: Current account balance. """ return self.balance # Example usage: account = BankAccount("1234567890", "John Doe", 1000.0) print(f"Initial balance: ${account.get_balance()}") account.deposit(500.0) account.withdraw(200.0) print(f"Final balance: ${account.get_balance()}") ``` This implementation includes: - A `BankAccount` class with constructor, deposit, withdrawal, and balance retrieval methods. - Type hints for method parameters and return types. - Docstrings explaining what each method does and its parameters. - Validation for deposit and withdrawal amounts. - Error messages for invalid operations. - Example usage demonstrating how to create an account, deposit, withdraw, and check the balance. user: Please modify your python class so it uses encapsulation. 16	system: # Retail agent policy As a retail agent, you can help users cancel or modify pending orders, return or exchange delivered orders, modify their default user address, or provide information about their own profile, orders, and related products. - At the beginning of the conversation, you have to authenticate the user identity by locating their user id via email, or via name + zip code. This has to be done even when the user already provides the user id. - Once the user has been authenticated, you can provide the user with information about order, product, profile information, e.g. help the user look up order id. - You can only help one user per conversation (but you can handle multiple requests from the same user), and must deny any requests for tasks related to any other user. - Before taking consequential actions that update the database (cancel, modify, return, exchange), you have to list the action detail and obtain explicit user confirmation (yes) to proceed. - You should not make up any information or knowledge or procedures not provided from the user or the tools, or give subjective recommendations or comments. - You should at most make one tool call at a time, and if you take a tool call, you should not respond to the user at the same time. If you respond to the user, you should not make a tool call. - You should transfer the user to a human agent if and only if the request cannot be handled within the scope of your actions. ## Domain basic - All times in the database are EST and 24 hour based. For example "02:30:00" means 2:30 AM EST. - Each user has a profile of its email, default address, user id, and payment methods. Each payment method is either a gift card, a paypal account, or a credit card. - Our retail store has 50 types of products. For each type of product, there are variant items of different options. For example, for a 't shirt' product, there could be an item with option 'color blue size M', and another item with option 'color red size L'. - Each product has an unique product id, and each item has an unique item id. They have no relations and should not be confused. - Each order can be in status 'pending', 'processed', 'delivered', or 'cancelled'. Generally, you can only take action on pending or delivered orders. - Exchange or modify order tools can only be called once. Be sure that all items to be changed are collected into a list before making the tool call!!! ## Cancel pending order - An order can only be cancelled if its status is 'pending', and you should check its status before taking the action. - The user needs to confirm the order id and the reason (either 'no longer needed' or 'ordered by mistake') for cancellation. - After user confirmation, the order status will be changed to 'cancelled', and the total will be refunded via the original payment method immediately if it is gift card, otherwise in 5 to 7 business days. ## Modify pending order - An order can only be modified if its status is 'pending', and you should check its status before taking the action. - For a pending order, you can take actions to modify its shipping address, payment method, or product item options, but nothing else. ### Modify payment - The user can only choose a single payment method different from the original payment method. - If the user wants the modify the payment method to gift card, it must have enough balance to cover the total amount. - After user confirmation, the order status will be kept 'pending'. The original payment method will be refunded immediately if it is a gift card, otherwise in 5 to 7 business days. ### Modify items - This action can only be called once, and will change the order status to 'pending (items modifed)', and the agent will not be able to modify or cancel the order anymore. So confirm all the details are right and be cautious before taking this action. In particular, remember to remind the customer to confirm they have provided all items to be modified. - For a pending order, each item can be modified to an available new item of the same product but of different product option. There cannot be any change of product types, e.g. modify shirt to shoe. - The user must provide a payment method to pay or receive refund of the price difference. If the user provides a gift card, it must have enough balance to cover the price difference. ## Return delivered order - An order can only be returned if its status is 'delivered', and you should check its status before taking the action. - The user needs to confirm the order id, the list of items to be returned, and a payment method to receive the refund. - The refund must either go to the original payment method, or an existing gift card. - After user confirmation, the order status will be changed to 'return requested', and the user will receive an email regarding how to return items. ## Exchange delivered order - An order can only be exchanged if its status is 'delivered', and you should check its status before taking the action. In particular, remember to remind the customer to confirm they have provided all items to be exchanged. - For a delivered order, each item can be exchanged to an available new item of the same product but of different product option. There cannot be any change of product types, e.g. modify shirt to shoe. - The user must provide a payment method to pay or receive refund of the price difference. If the user provides a gift card, it must have enough balance to cover the price difference. - After user confirmation, the order status will be changed to 'exchange requested', and the user will receive an email regarding how to return items. This has to be done even when the user already provides the user id. - Once the user has been authenticated, you can provide the user with information about order, product, profile information, e.g. help the user look up order id. - You can only help one user per conversation (but you can handle multiple requests from the same user), and must deny any requests for tasks related to any other user. - Before taking consequential actions that update the database (cancel, modify, return, exchange), you have to list the action detail and obtain explicit user confirmation (yes) to proceed. - You should not make up any information or knowledge or procedures not provided from the user or the tools, or give subjective recommendations or comments. - You should at most make one tool call at a time, and if you take a tool call, you should not respond to the user at the same time. If you respond to the user, you should not make a tool call. - You should transfer the user to a human agent if and only if the request cannot be handled within the scope of your actions. ## Domain basic - All times in the database are EST and 24 hour based. For example "02:30:00" means 2:30 AM EST. - Each user has a profile of its email, default address, user id, and payment methods. Each payment method is either a gift card, a paypal account, or a credit card. - Our retail store has 50 types of products. For each type of product, there are variant items of different options. For example, for a 't shirt' product, there could be an item with option 'color blue size M', and another item with option 'color red size L'. - Each product has an unique product id, and each item has an unique item id. They have no relations and should not be confused. - Each order can be in status 'pending', 'processed', 'delivered', or 'cancelled'. Generally, you can only take action on pending or delivered orders. - Exchange or modify order tools can only be called once. Be sure that all items to be changed are collected into a list before making the tool call!!! ## Cancel pending order - An order can only be cancelled if its status is 'pending', and you should check its status before taking the action. - The user needs to confirm the order id and the reason (either 'no longer needed' or 'ordered by mistake') for cancellation. - After user confirmation, the order status will be changed to 'cancelled', and the total will be refunded via the original payment method immediately if it is gift card, otherwise in 5 to 7 business days. ## Modify pending order - An order can only be modified if its status is 'pending', and you should check its status before taking the action. - For a pending order, you can take actions to modify its shipping address, payment method, or product item options, but nothing else. ### Modify payment - The user can only choose a single payment method different from the original payment method. - If the user wants the modify the payment method to gift card, it must have enough balance to cover the total amount. - After user confirmation, the order status will be kept 'pending'. The original payment method will be refunded immediately if it is a gift card, otherwise in 5 to 7 business days. ### Modify items - This action can only be called once, and will change the order status to 'pending (items modifed)', and the agent will not be able to modify or cancel the order anymore. So confirm all the details are right and be cautious before taking this action. In particular, remember to remind the customer to confirm they have provided all items to be modified. - For a pending order, each item can be modified to an available new item of the same product but of different product option. There cannot be any change of product types, e.g. modify shirt to shoe. - The user must provide a payment method to pay or receive refund of the price difference. If the user provides a gift card, it must have enough balance to cover the price difference. ## Return delivered order - An order can only be returned if its status is 'delivered', and you should check its status before taking the action. - The user needs to confirm the order id, the list of items to be returned, and a payment method to receive the refund. - The refund must either go to the original payment method, or an existing gift card. - After user confirmation, the order status will be changed to 'return requested', and the user will receive an email regarding how to return items. ## Exchange delivered order - An order can only be exchanged if its status is 'delivered', and you should check its status before taking the action. In particular, remember to remind the customer to confirm they have provided all items to be exchanged. - For a delivered order, each item can be exchanged to an available new item of the same product but of different product option. There cannot be any change of product types, e.g. modify shirt to shoe. - The user must provide a payment method to pay or receive refund of the price difference. If the user provides a gift card, it must have enough balance to cover the price difference. - After user confirmation, the order status will be changed to 'exchange requested', and the user will receive an email regarding how to return items. 33	user: Heidi enjoyed performing the simulation because she knew exactly when a new universe would be formed and where, and when a non-existent link would be broken and where. However, the multiverse itself works in mysterious ways. Well, it works using probabilities, which to some people is mysterious. At each unit time, when a decision is made, one of the two events will happen randomly. Let's denote l as the current length of the multiverse. With a probability of p_{create} = 1 - l/m, a universe will be created. With a probability of p_{break}=l/m, a non-existent link will be broken at some position. More specifically, * When a universe is created, it will manifest itself between any two adjacent universes or at one of the ends. Each position occurs with a probability of (1)/(l + 1). * When a link is broken, it could be cut between any two adjacent universes, each with a probability of (1)/(l-1). After separating the multiverse into two segments, the segment NOT containing the Doctor will cease to exist. As earlier, the Doctor remains in the same universe. However, if at some point the multiverse breaks in such a way that the Doctor finds himself at the leftmost or rightmost end of it, the TARDIS stops functioning. In such a case, the Doctor must actually walk across the multiverse to find the tools to fix it. We are interested in the expected value of the length of the multiverse when such an event occurs. Input The first and only line contains three integers n, k and m (1 ≤ k ≤ n ≤ m ≤ 250), the initial length of the multiverse, the initial position of the Doctor, and the maximum possible length of the multiverse. Output Output a single integer on a single line, indicating the expected length of the multiverse. If the answer is p/q, please print r where p ≡ r ⋅ q (mod 10^9 + 7). For the third test case, the multiverse can only break at a position, which renders the Doctor at one of its ends. For the fourth case, things seem to be a little more complicated, because the multiverse can grow and then be broken again. assistant: ```python MOD = 10**9 + 7 def main(): import sys n, k, m = map(int, sys.stdin.readline().split()) max_inv_needed = m + 1 # since L can be up to m, and L+1 can be m+1 # Precompute inverses up to max_inv_needed inv = [0] * (max_inv_needed + 1) for i in range(1, max_inv_needed + 1): inv[i] = pow(i, MOD-2, MOD) # Initialize E max_length = m E = [[0] * (max_length + 2) for _ in range(max_length + 2)] for L in range(1, max_length + 1): E[L][1] = L % MOD E[L][L] = L % MOD # Process L from m down to 1 for L in range(max_length, 0, -1): # Iterate over positions from 2 to L-1 inclusive for pos in range(2, L): create_contribution = 0 if L < m: sum_Create = 0 for I in range(0, L + 1): if pos == 0: new_p = 0 else: new_p = pos + 1 if I < pos else pos new_L = L + 1 sum_Create = (sum_Create + E[new_L][new_p]) % MOD denom = L + 1 inv_denom = inv[denom] term = (sum_Create * inv_denom) % MOD numerator_factor = (m - L) % MOD inv_m = inv[m] prod = (numerator_factor * inv_m) % MOD create_contribution = (term * prod) % MOD else: create_contribution = 0 # can't create any more break_contribution = 0 sum_break_terms = 0 for x in range(1, L): # x from 1 to L-1 inclusive if pos <= x: new_L_l = x new_p_l = pos if new_p_l == 1 or new_p_l == new_L_l: val = new_L_l % MOD else: val = E[new_L_l][new_p_l] else: new_L_r = L - We use dynamic programming (DP) to model the problem, considering both universe creation and link breaking events. ### Approach 1. **State Definition**: Define `dp[l][k]` as the expected length when the multiverse has length `l` and the Doctor is at position `k`. 2. **Base Case**: If the Doctor is already at one of the ends (`k == 1` or `k == l`), the expected value is `l` directly. 3. **Transitions**: - **Universe Creation**: With probability `p_create = (m-l)/m`, the multiverse length increases by 1. The Doctor's new position depends on where the universe is inserted. - **Link Breaking**: With probability `p_break = l/m`, the multiverse splits, and we consider all possible splits to compute the expected value correctly. 4. **Modular Arithmetic**: Use modular inverses to handle divisions under modulo `10^9 + 7`. ### Solution Code ```python MOD = 10**9 + 7 def main(): import sys n, k_initial, m = map(int, sys.stdin.readline().split()) max_l = m # Initialize DP table dp = [[0]*(max_l +2) for _ in range(max_l +2)] # dp[l][k] for l in range(1, max_l +1): for k in range(1, l+1): if k == 1 or k == l: dp[l][k] = l % MOD # Process from max_l down to 1 for current_l in range(max_l, 0, -1): for current_k in range(2, current_l): # Compute E(current_l, current_k) # First compute p_create and E_crt part numerator_pcr = (m - current_l) % MOD inv_m = pow(m, MOD-2, MOD) pcr_coeff = (numerator_pcr * inv_m) % MOD E_crt_val = 0 l_plus = current_l + 1 if l_plus <= max_l: k_plus = current_k + 1 k_same = current_k term1 = (current_k * dp[l_plus][k_plus]) % MOD term2 = (( (l_plus - current_k) ) * dp[l_plus][k_same]) % MOD numerator = (term1 + term2) % MOD inv_denom_crt = pow(l_plus, MOD-2, MOD) E_crt_val = (numerator * inv_denom_crt) % MOD E_crt_part = (pcr_coeff * E_crt_val) % MOD # Compute E_break part pbr_coeff = (current_l * inv_m) % MOD l_minus1 = current_l -1 if l_minus1 ==0: # current_l is 1, but there are no possible k in this case E_break_contribution =0 else: sum_terms =0 inv_denom_br = pow(l_minus1, MOD-2, MOD) for s in range(1, current_l): if s >= current_k: new_L = s new_k = current_k else: new_L = current_l - s new_k	user: Heidi enjoyed performing the simulation because she knew exactly when a new universe would be formed and where, and when a non-existent link would be broken and where. However, the multiverse itself works in mysterious ways. Well, it works using probabilities, which to some people is mysterious. At each unit time, when a decision is made, one of the two events will happen randomly. Let's denote l as the current length of the multiverse. After separating the multiverse into two segments, the segment NOT containing the Doctor will cease to exist. As earlier, the Doctor remains in the same universe. Output Output a single integer on a single line, indicating the expected length of the multiverse. If the answer is p/q, please print r where p ≡ r ⋅ q (mod 10^9 + 7). Examples Input 2 1 2 Output 2 Input 2 2 10 Output 2 Input 3 2 3 Output 2 Input 3 2 5 Output 941659828 Input 10 4 20 Output 196683114 Note For the first and the second test case, without any change to the multiverse, the Doctor is already at one of the ends. For the third test case, the multiverse can only break at a position, which renders the Doctor at one of its ends. For the fourth case, things seem to be a little more complicated, because the multiverse can grow and then be broken again. assistant: To solve the problem of finding the expected length of the multiverse when the Doctor ends up at the end, we model this scenario using dynamic programming. We define `dp[L][p]` as the expected value of the multiverse's length when the current length is `L` and the Doctor's position is `p`. ### Approach 1. **Dynamic Programming Table Initialization**: - The base cases are when the Doctor is at either end of the multiverse (`p = 1` or `p = L`), in which case the expected length is `L`. 2. **Reverse Order Processing**: - Process the states starting from the maximum possible length down to 1. This allows us to compute values for smaller lengths using results from larger lengths. 3. **Transition Cases**: - **Creation Event**: When a new universe is created, the length becomes `L+1`, and the new position of the Doctor depends on where the insertion occurs. This is handled using weighted averages based on the possible insertion points. - **Break Event**: When a link is broken, the multiverse can be split into two segments. We compute the contributions from each possible split considering whether the Doctor is in the left or right segment and if the resulting position is at an end. 4. **Modular Arithmetic**: - Handle all calculations modulo `10^9 + 7` using modular inverses for division. ### Solution Code ```python MOD = 10**9 + 7 def main(): import sys n, k, m_val = map(int, sys.stdin.readline().split()) max_L = m_val # Initialize DP table dp = [[0] * (max_L + 2) for _ in range(max_L + 2)] for L in range(1, max_L + 1): for p in range(1, L + 1): if p == 1 or p == L: dp[L][p] = L % MOD else: dp[L][p] = 0 # placeholder, will be computed later # Process L from max down to 1 for L in range(max_L, 0, -1): for p in range(2, L): # p from 2 to L-1 (since range is exclusive on upper) create_cont = 0 if L < max_L: numerator = (m_val - L) % MOD denom_m = m_val next_L = L + 1 E_plus1 = dp[next_L][p + 1] E_p = dp[next_L][p] # Compute numerator_part = p * E_plus1 + (next_L - p) * E_p part1 = (p * E_plus1) % MOD part2 = (( (next_L - p) * E_p ) % MOD) numerator_part = (part1 + part2) % MOD numerator_total = (numerator * numerator_part) % MOD denom_total = (denom_m * next_L) % MOD inv_denom = pow(denom_total, MOD-2, MOD) create_cont = (numerator_total * inv_denom) % MOD break_cont = 0 if L > 1: s = 0 for x in range(1, L): if p <= x: new_L_caseA = x new_p = p if p == x: val = x % MOD else: val = dp[new_L_caseA][new_p] % MOD else: diff = p - x new_L_caseB = L - x new_p_caseB = diff if diff == 1: val = (L - x) % MOD else: val = dp[new_L_caseB][new_p_caseB] % MOD s = (s + val) % MOD denominator_break = L - 1 numerator_coeff = L % MOD inv_denominator_break = pow(denominator_break, MOD-2, MOD) inv_m = pow(m_val, MOD-2, MOD) multiplier = ( (numerator_coeff * inv_m) % MOD ) * inv_denominator_break % MOD break_cont = (s * multiplier) % MOD dp[L][p] = (create_cont + break_cont) % MOD print(dp[n][k] % MOD) if __name__ == "__main__": main() ``` ### Explanation - **Initialization**: We initialize the dynamic programming table `dp` with base cases where the Doctor is already at the end. - **Reverse Order Processing**: We process each possible length `L` from the maximum down to the minimum to ensure we can use computed values from previous steps. - **Create Event**: We compute the expected value for creating a new universe by considering all possible insertion points and their impact on the Doctor's position. - **Break Event**: For each possible break point, we determine the resulting segments and whether the Doctor is now at an end, using modular arithmetic to handle divisions. This approach efficiently computes the expected value using dynamic programming and handles all transitions and modular operations correctly to ensure the solution is both correct and optimal.

Ray is a segment which connects point on boundary of the ellipse with some point outside ellipse. <image> An image where all suns are circles. <image> An image where all suns are ellipses, their axes are parallel to the coordinate axes. <image> An image where all suns are rotated ellipses. It is guaranteed that: * No two suns have common points. * The rays’ width is 3 pixels. * The lengths of the ellipsis suns’ axes will lie between 40 and 200 pixels. * No two rays intersect. * The lengths of all rays will lie between 10 and 30 pixels. Input The first line contains two integers h and w — the height and width of the image (1 ≤ h, w ≤ 1600). Next h lines will contain w space-separated integers each. They describe Smart Beaver’s picture. Each number equals either a 0 (the image background), or a 1 (the sun color). The input limits for scoring 30 points are (subproblem F1): * All suns on the image are circles. The input limits for scoring 70 points are (subproblems F1+F2): * All suns on the image are ellipses with axes parallel to the coordinate axes. The input limits for scoring 100 points are (subproblems F1+F2+F3): * All suns on the image are ellipses, they can be arbitrarily rotated. Output The first line must contain a single number k — the number of suns on the beaver’s image. The second line must contain exactly k space-separated integers, corresponding to the number of rays on each sun. The numbers of the second line must be sorted in the increasing order. After three erosions, the remaining pixels form the core	user: The Smart Beaver from ABBYY plans a space travel on an ultramodern spaceship. During the voyage he plans to visit n planets. For planet i ai is the maximum number of suitcases that an alien tourist is allowed to bring to the planet, and bi is the number of citizens on the planet. The Smart Beaver is going to bring some presents from ABBYY to the planets he will be visiting. The presents are packed in suitcases, x presents in each. The Beaver will take to the ship exactly a1 + ... + an suitcases. As the Beaver lands on the i-th planet, he takes ai suitcases and goes out. On the first day on the planet the Beaver takes a walk and gets to know the citizens. On the second and all subsequent days the Beaver gives presents to the citizens — each of the bi citizens gets one present per day. The Beaver leaves the planet in the evening of the day when the number of presents left is strictly less than the number of citizens (i.e. as soon as he won't be able to give away the proper number of presents the next day). He leaves the remaining presents at the hotel. The Beaver is going to spend exactly c days traveling. The time spent on flights between the planets is considered to be zero. In how many ways can one choose the positive integer x so that the planned voyage will take exactly c days? Input The first input line contains space-separated integers n and c — the number of planets that the Beaver is going to visit and the number of days he is going to spend traveling, correspondingly. The next n lines contain pairs of space-separated integers ai, bi (1 ≤ i ≤ n) — the number of suitcases he can bring to the i-th planet and the number of citizens of the i-th planet, correspondingly. The input limitations for getting 30 points are: * 1 ≤ n ≤ 100 * 1 ≤ ai ≤ 100 * 1 ≤ bi ≤ 100 * 1 ≤ c ≤ 100 The input limitations for getting 100 points are: * 1 ≤ n ≤ 104 * 0 ≤ ai ≤ 109 * 1 ≤ bi ≤ 109 * 1 ≤ c ≤ 109 Due to possible overflow, it is recommended to use the 64-bit arithmetic. In some solutions even the 64-bit arithmetic can overflow. So be careful in calculations! Output Print a single number k — the number of ways to choose x so as to travel for exactly c days. If there are infinitely many possible values of x, print -1. Please do not use the %lld specifier to read or write 64-bit integers in С++. It is preferred to use cin, cout streams or the %I64d specifier. Examples Input 2 5 1 5 2 4 Output 1 Note In the first example there is only one suitable value x = 5. Then the Beaver takes 1 suitcase with 5 presents to the first planet. Here he spends 2 days: he hangs around on the first day, and he gives away five presents on the second day. He takes 2 suitcases with 10 presents to the second planet. Here he spends 3 days — he gives away 4 presents on the second and the third days and leaves the remaining 2 presents at the hotel. In total, the Beaver spends 5 days traveling. For x = 4 or less the Beaver won't have enough presents for the second day on the first planet, so the voyage will end too soon. For x = 6 and more the Beaver will spend at least one more day on the second planet, and the voyage will take too long. assistant: ```python import sys def main(): n, c = map(int, sys.stdin.readline().split()) a_list = [] for _ in range(n): a, b = map(int, sys.stdin.readline().split()) if a > 0: a_list.append( (a, b) ) # Check if all a's are zero n_terms = len(a_list) if n_terms == 0: if c == n: print(-1) else: print(0) return T = c - n if T < 0: print(0) return def compute(x): res = 0 for a, b in a_list: res += (a * x) // b return res # Function to find x_low: first x where f(x) >= T def find_x_low(T_val): low, high = 1, 10**18 x_low = 10**18 + 1 while low <= high: mid = (low + high) // 2 v = compute(mid) if v >= T_val: x_low = mid high = mid -1 else: low = mid +1 return x_low # Function to find x_high: first x where f(x) > T def find_x_high(T_val): low, high = 1, 10**18 x_high = 10**18 +1 while low <= high: mid = (low + high) //2 v = compute(mid) if v > T_val: x_high = mid high = mid -1 else: low = mid +1 return x_high x_low = find_x_low(T) x_high = find_x_high(T) if compute(x_low) != T: print(0) else: ans = x_high - x_low if ans <0: print(0) else: print(ans) if __name__ == "__main__": main() ```	user: Smart Beaver became interested in drawing. He draws suns. However, at some point, Smart Beaver realized that simply drawing suns is boring. So he decided to design a program that will process his drawings. Input The first line contains two integers h and w — the height and width of the image (1 ≤ h, w ≤ 1600). Next h lines will contain w space-separated integers each. They describe Smart Beaver’s picture. Each number equals either a 0 (the image background), or a 1 (the sun color). The input limits for scoring 30 points are (subproblem F1): * All suns on the image are circles. The input limits for scoring 70 points are (subproblems F1+F2): * All suns on the image are ellipses with axes parallel to the coordinate axes. Examples Note For each complexity level you are suggested a sample in the initial data. You can download the samples at http://www.abbyy.ru/sun.zip. assistant: To solve this problem, we need to identify the number of suns in a given image and count the number of rays each sun has. The suns are represented as ellipses with rays extending from them, and the image is given as a grid of 0s and 1s, where 1s represent parts of suns. ### Approach 1. **Identify Connected Components**: - Use BFS/DFS to find all connected components of 1s, where each component represents a sun along with its rays. 2. **Extract Boundary Points**: - For each component (sun), determine the boundary points that form the outline of the sun. These are the points in the component that are adjacent to a background pixel (0). 3. **Fit an Ellipse to the Boundary**: - Compute the covariance matrix of the boundary points to determine the ellipse parameters (center, axes lengths, and orientation) using PCA. - Use eigen decomposition to compute the major and minor axes and the orientation of the ellipse. 4. **Classify Points**: - Using the fitted ellipse parameters, classify each point in the component as being either inside or outside the ellipse. Points outside the ellipse are considered part of the rays. 5. **Count Rays**: - The points identified as part of the rays form connected regions. Using BFS/DFS, count these regions to determine the number of rays for each sun. ### Solution Code ```python import sys from collections import deque import math def main(): h, w = map(int, sys.stdin.readline().split()) image = [] for _ in range(h): row = list(map(int, sys.stdin.readline().split())) image.append(row) visited = [[False for _ in range(w)] for _ in range(h)] components = [] # Find all connected components (suns) for i in range(h): for j in range(w): if image[i][j] == 1 and not visited[i][j]: # BFS to find the component component = [] q = deque() q.append((i, j)) visited[i][j] = True component.append((i, j)) while q: x, y = q.popleft() for dx in (-1,0,1): for dy in (-1,0,1): nx, ny = x+dx, y+dy if 0 <= nx < h and 0 <= ny < w: if not visited[nx][ny] and image[nx][ny] ==1: visited[nx][ny] = True component.append((nx, ny)) q.append((nx, ny)) components.append(component) ray_counts = [] for comp in components: # Step 2: Compute boundary points of the component boundary = [] comp_set = set(comp) for (x,y) in comp: is_b = False for dx in (-1,0,1): for dy in (-1,0,1): if dx ==0 and dy ==0: continue nx, ny = x + dx, y + dy if 0<= nx <h and 0<= ny <w: if image[nx][ny] ==0: is_b = True break else: is_b = True break if is_b: break if is_b: boundary.append( (x,y) ) if not boundary: continue # Step 3: get ellipse parameters # Compute mean of boundary points N = len(boundary) sum_x = 0 sum_y =0 for (x,y) in boundary: sum_x +=x sum_y +=y cx = sum_x / N cy = sum_y / N dxs = [] dys = [] for (x,y) in boundary: dx = x - cx dy = y - cy dxs.append(dx) dys.append(dy) # compute the covariance matrix cov_xx = 0.0 cov_xy =0.0 cov_yy= 0.0 for dx, dy in zip(dxs, dys): cov_xx += dx * dx cov_xy += dx * dy cov_yy += dy * dy cov_xx /= N cov_xy /= N cov_yy /= N A = cov_xx C = cov_xy B = cov_yy # compute eigenvalues avg = (A + B)/2.0 discriminant = (A - B)**2 + (2*C)**2 sqrt_d = math.sqrt(discriminant) /2.0 lambda1 = avg + sqrt_d lambda2 = avg - sqrt_d if lambda2 > lambda1: lambda1, lambda2 = lambda2, lambda1 # Find eigenvector for lambda1, which is major. # Using equations to find eigenvector (vx, vy) # For (A - lambda)v.x + C v.y = 0 # We can choose a direction vector (C, (lambda1 - A)) vx = C vy = lambda1 - A length = math.hypot(vx, vy) if length ==0: # degenerate, then just pick (1,0) vx, vy =1,0 else: vx /= length vy /= length # angle in radians theta = math.atan2(vy, vx) # semi-axis scaled by sqrt(5.991 * lambda) scaling = 5.991 # for 95% confidence ellipse a_sq = scaling * lambda1 b_sq = scaling * lambda2 a_sq = max(a_sq, 1e-6) b_sq = max(b_sq, 1e-6) a = math.sqrt(a_sq) b = math.sqrt(b_sq) # Step4: points in the component not inside the ellipse are in the ray. # Now compute mask comp_xys = set(comp) ray_points = [] for (x,y) in comp: dx = x - cx dy = y - cy rot_x = dx * math.cos(theta) + dy * math.sin(theta) rot_y = -dx * math.sin(theta) + dy * math.cos(theta) val = (rot_x**2)/a_sq + (rot_y**2)/b_sq if val >1: ray_points.append( (x,y) ) # count connected regions in ray_points # We need to BFS over these points # Using a set for quick existence check: ray_set = set(ray_points) visited_mask = [[False for _ in range(w)] for _ in range(h)] count =0 for (x,y) in ray_points: if not visited_mask[x][y]: count +=1 q = deque() q.append( (x,y) ) visited_mask[x][y] = True while q: x0, y0 = q.popleft() for dx in (-1,0,1): for dy in (-1,0,1): nx, ny = x0+dx, y0+dy if 0<= nx <h and 0<= ny <w and (nx,ny) in ray_set and not visited_mask[nx][ny]: visited_mask[nx][ny] = True q.append( (nx, ny) ) ray_counts.append(count) ray_counts.sort() print(len(ray_counts)) if ray_counts: print(' '.join(map(str, ray_counts))) else: print() if __name__ == "__main__": main() ``` ### Explanation 1. **Connected Components**: Each sun is identified by traversing all connected 1s using BFS. 2. **Boundary Extraction**: For each component, boundary points are identified as those adjacent to any 0. 3. **Ellipse Fitting**: The ellipse is determined using PCA on boundary points, computing covariance matrix eigenvalues and eigenvectors. 4. **Ray Classification**: Points outside the fitted ellipse in the component are considered rays. 5. **Ray Counting**: Connected regions of these points are counted using BFS, giving the number of rays per sun. Results are sorted and outputted.
28	user: Lunar rover finally reached planet X. After landing, he met an obstacle, that contains permutation p of length n. Scientists found out, that to overcome an obstacle, the robot should make p an identity permutation (make p_i = i for all i). Unfortunately, scientists can't control the robot. Thus the only way to make p an identity permutation is applying the following operation to p multiple times: * Select two indices i and j (i ≠ j), such that p_j = i and swap the values of p_i and p_j. It takes robot (j - i)^2 seconds to do this operation. Positions i and j are selected by the robot (scientists can't control it). He will apply this operation while p isn't an identity permutation. We can show that the robot will make no more than n operations regardless of the choice of i and j on each operation. Scientists asked you to find out the maximum possible time it will take the robot to finish making p an identity permutation (i. e. worst-case scenario), so they can decide whether they should construct a new lunar rover or just rest and wait. They won't believe you without proof, so you should build an example of p and robot's operations that maximizes the answer. For a better understanding of the statement, read the sample description. Input The first line of input contains a single integer t (1 ≤ t ≤ 10^4) — the number of test cases. Each of next t lines contains the single integer n (2 ≤ n ≤ 10^5) – the length of p. Note, that p is not given to you. You should find the maximum possible time over all permutations of length n. It is guaranteed, that the total sum of n over all test cases doesn't exceed 10^5. Output For each test case in the first line, print how many seconds will the robot spend in the worst case. In the next line, print the initial value of p that you used to construct an answer. In the next line, print the number of operations m ≤ n that the robot makes in your example. In the each of next m lines print two integers i and j — indices of positions that the robot will swap on this operation. Note that p_j = i must holds (at the time of operation). Example Input 3 2 3 3 Output 1 2 1 1 2 1 5 2 3 1 2 1 3 3 2 5 2 3 1 2 1 3 2 3 Note For n = 2, p can be either [1, 2] or [2, 1]. In the first case p is already identity, otherwise robot will make it an identity permutation in 1 second regardless of choise i and j on the first operation. For n = 3, p can be equals [2, 3, 1]. * If robot will select i = 3, j = 2 on the first operation, p will become [2, 1, 3] in one second. Now robot can select only i = 1, j = 2 or i = 2, j = 1. In both cases, p will become identity in one more second (2 seconds in total). * If robot will select i = 1, j = 3 on the first operation, p will become [1, 3, 2] in four seconds. Regardless of choise of i and j on the second operation, p will become identity in five seconds. We can show, that for permutation of length 3 robot will always finish all operation in no more than 5 seconds. assistant: ```python import sys def main(): input = sys.stdin.read().split() idx = 0 t = int(input[idx]) idx += 1 for _ in range(t): n = int(input[idx]) idx += 1 # Compute the maximum time which is sum of squares from 1^2 to (n-1)^2 total_time = (n - 1) * n * (2 * n - 1) // 6 print(total_time) # The permutation is [2,3,...,n,1] perm = list(range(2, n+1)) + [1] print(' '.join(map(str, perm))) m = n -1 print(m) for i in range(1, n): print(f"{i} {n}") if __name__ == "__main__": main() ```	user: Lunar rover finally reached planet X. Example Input 3 2 3 3 Output 1 2 1 1 2 1 5 2 3 1 2 1 3 3 2 5 2 3 1 2 1 3 2 3 Note For n = 2, p can be either [1, 2] or [2, 1]. In the first case p is already identity, otherwise robot will make it an identity permutation in 1 second regardless of choise i and j on the first operation. For n = 3, p can be equals [2, 3, 1]. * If robot will select i = 3, j = 2 on the first operation, p will become [2, 1, 3] in one second. Now robot can select only i = 1, j = 2 or i = 2, j = 1. In both cases, p will become identity in one more second (2 seconds in total). * If robot will select i = 1, j = 3 on the first operation, p will become [1, 3, 2] in four seconds. Regardless of choise of i and j on the second operation, p will become identity in five seconds. We can show, that for permutation of length 3 robot will always finish all operation in no more than 5 seconds. assistant: To solve this problem, we need to determine the maximum possible time a lunar rover will take to turn a permutation into an identity permutation using specific swap operations. The key insight is that the worst-case scenario occurs when the permutation is a single cycle of maximum length, allowing the robot to use the swaps with the maximum possible distances each time. ### Approach 1. **Understanding the Swap Operations**: The robot can swap elements at positions `i` and `j` if `p_j = i`, and each such swap takes `(j - i)^2` seconds. The goal is to maximize the total time taken. 2. **Cycle Structure**: The worst-case permutation is a single cycle of length `n` (e.g., `[2, 3, 4, ..., n, 1]`), which allows for the maximum possible swaps with the largest distances. 3. **Maximizing Time**: By breaking down the cycle optimally, each swap between a position and the last position, starting from the first element and moving towards the end, yields the maximum possible distance each time. This results in a series of swaps where each subsequent swap is between the next position and the last, ensuring the total time is the sum of squares of distances from 1 to `n-1`. The maximum time is calculated using the formula for the sum of squares of the first `n-1` integers: \[ \text{Sum} = \frac{(n-1) \cdot n \cdot (2n - 1)}{6} \] ### Solution Code ```python def main(): import sys input = sys.stdin.read().split() t = int(input[0]) idx = 1 for _ in range(t): n = int(input[idx]) idx += 1 # Compute the sum: 1^2 + 2^2 + ... + (n-1)^2 = (n-1)*n*(2n-1)//6 sum_squares = (n - 1) * n * (2 * n - 1) // 6 # Generate permutation: [2, 3, ..., n, 1] perm = list(range(2, n+1)) + [1] m = n - 1 print(sum_squares) print(' '.join(map(str, perm))) print(m) for i in range(1, n): print(i, n) if __name__ == "__main__": main() ``` ### Explanation 1. **Reading Input**: The input is read in one go for efficiency, especially given the constraints. 2. **Calculating Maximum Time**: Using the formula for the sum of squares of the first `n-1` integers ensures we compute the worst-case time efficiently. 3. **Constructing the Permutation**: The permutation is constructed as a single cycle `[2, 3, ..., n, 1]`, which ensures all elements form a single cycle. 4. **Generating Swaps**: The swaps are generated sequentially between each position from 1 to `n-1` and the last position `n`, ensuring each swap uses the maximum possible distance at each step. This approach efficiently computes the required values and ensures the worst-case permutation and swap sequence are correctly generated, providing the solution within the problem constraints.
34	user: A telecommunications company is planning to expand its network coverage by installing new cell towers across a rural area. The company needs to cover a total area of 10,000 square miles, and each cell tower has a coverage radius of 5 miles. Calculate the minimum number of cell towers needed to provide complete coverage, and estimate the cost of this project, given that each cell tower costs $300,000 to install and requires an annual maintenance budget of $50,000. assistant: First, we need to calculate the area covered by each cell tower. The formula for the area of a circle is A = πr^2, where A is the area and r is the radius. A = π(5 miles)^2 A ≈ 3.14159 * 25 square miles A ≈ 78.54 square miles Now, we can find out how many cell towers are needed to cover the entire rural area: Number of cell towers = Total area / Area covered by one cell tower Number of cell towers = 10,000 square miles / 78.54 square miles Number of cell towers ≈ 127.32 Since we cannot have a fraction of a cell tower, we will round up to the nearest whole number, which means we need at least 128 cell towers to provide complete coverage. Next, let's estimate the cost of installing these cell towers: Installation cost = Number of cell towers * Cost per cell tower Installation cost = 128 * $300,000 Installation cost = $38,400,000 Finally, let's estimate the annual maintenance budget for all the cell towers: Annual maintenance budget = Number of cell towers * Maintenance budget per cell tower Annual maintenance budget = 128 * $50,000 Annual maintenance budget = $6,400,000 So, the minimum number of cell towers needed is 128, with an installation cost of $38,400,000 and an annual maintenance budget of $6,400,000.	user: After making a strategic plan with carriers for expansion of mobile network throughout the whole country, the government decided to cover rural areas with the last generation of 5G network. Since 5G antenna towers will be built in the area of mainly private properties, the government needs an easy way to find information about landowners for each property partially or fully contained in the planned building area. The planned building area is represented as a rectangle with sides width and height. Every 5G antenna tower occupies a circle with a center in (x,y) and radius r. There is a database of Geodetic Institute containing information about each property. Each property is defined with its identification number and polygon represented as an array of (x,y) points in the counter-clockwise direction. Your task is to build an IT system which can handle queries of type (x, y, r) in which (x,y) represents a circle center, while r represents its radius. The IT system should return the total area of properties that need to be acquired for the building of a tower so that the government can estimate the price. Furthermore, the system should return a list of identification numbers of these properties (so that the owners can be contacted for land acquisition). A property needs to be acquired if the circle of the antenna tower is intersecting or touching it. Input The first line contains the size of the building area as double values width, height, and an integer n — the number of properties in the database. Each of the next n lines contains the description of a single property in the form of an integer number v (3 ≤ v ≤ 40) — the number of points that define a property, as well as 2*v double numbers — the coordinates (x,y) of each property point. Line i (0 ≤ i ≤ n-1) contains the information for property with id i. The next line contains an integer q — the number of queries. Precision being used for solution checking is 10^{-4}. assistant: ```cpp #include <bits/stdc++.h> using namespace std; using Point = pair<double, double>; struct Property { vector<Point> vertices; double area; double min_x, max_x; double min_y, max_y; }; const double INF = 1e10; const double EPS = 1e-8; inline double sq(double x) { return x * x; } double computePolygonArea(const vector<Point>& poly) { double area = 0.0; int n = poly.size(); for (int i = 0; i < n; ++i) { auto a = poly[i]; auto b = poly[(i + 1) % n]; area += (a.first * b.second - b.first * a.second); } return std::abs(area) * 0.5; } void computeBoundingBox(const vector<Point>& poly, Property& prop) { prop.min_x = INF; prop.max_x = -INF; prop.min_y = INF; prop.max_y = -INF; for (const auto& p : poly) { prop.min_x = min(prop.min_x, p.first); prop.max_x = max(prop.max_x, p.first); prop.min_y = min(prop.min_y, p.second); prop.max_y = max(prop.max_y, p.second); } } bool pointInPolygon(const vector<Point>& poly, double px, double py) { bool inside = false; int n = poly.size(); for (int i = 0; i < n; ++i) { auto a = poly[i]; auto b = poly[(i + 1) % n]; double x1 = a.first, y1 = a.second; double x2 = b.first, y2 = b.second; double y1_low = y1 <= py + EPS; double y2_low = y2 <= py + EPS; double between_y = (y1_low ^ y2_low); if (between_y) { double d_y = y2 - y1; if (std::abs(d_y) < EPS) continue; double t = (py - y1) / d_y; double x_intersect = x1 + t * (x2 - x1); if (px < x_intersect + EPS) { inside = !inside; } } } return inside; } bool edgeCheck(Property& prop, double cx, double cy, double r_sq) { const auto&	user: After making a strategic plan with carriers for expansion of mobile network throughout the whole country, the government decided to cover rural areas with the last generation of 5G network. Example Input 10 10 3 4 2 2 3 2 3 3 2 3 3 3.5 2 4.5 2 4.5 3 4 7 8 7.5 8.5 8 8 7.5 9 5 2 3.5 0.5 3.3 2 0.4 5 2.5 0.5 7.5 8.5 0.5 3 7 0.5 Output 1.000000 1 0 1.500000 2 0 1 0.500000 1 1 0.250000 1 2 0.000000 0 Note You can assume that the land not covered with properties (polygons) is under the government's ownership and therefore doesn't need to be acquired. Properties do not intersect with each other. Precision being used for solution checking is 10^{-4}. assistant: To solve this problem, we need to determine the total area of properties that intersect with a given circle (antenna tower coverage area) for multiple queries. Each property is represented as a polygon, and we need to efficiently check each polygon against multiple queries to determine if they intersect and compute the required areas. ### Approach 1. **Preprocessing Polygons**: - **Store each property's vertices and compute its area** using the shoelace formula. This allows us to quickly look up the area once a polygon is identified. 2. **Query Processing**: - For each query (circle center and radius): - **Check if any vertex** of a polygon is within the circle using simple distance calculations. - If no vertices are found, **check each edge** of the polygon to see if it intersects with the circle using line-segment to circle intersection checks. This is done efficiently by finding the closest point on the edge segment to the circle's center and checking if this distance is within the radius. ### Solution Code ```cpp #include <iostream> #include <vector> #include <cmath> #include <iomanip> #include <cstdio> using namespace std; struct Polygon { int id; double area; vector<pair<double, double>> vertices; }; int main() { ios::sync_with_stdio(false); cin.tie(nullptr); double width, height; int n; cin >> width >> height >> n; vector<Polygon> polygons; polygons.reserve(n); for (int i = 0; i < n; ++i) { int v; cin >> v; vector<pair<double, double>> vertices(v); for (auto& p : vertices) { cin >> p.first >> p.second; } Polygon poly; poly.id = i; poly.vertices = std::move(vertices); // Compute area using shoelace formula double sum = 0.0; for (int k = 0; k < v; ++k) {
